\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

- \(x_1 = 5\), \(y_1 = 0\)
- \(x_2 = 9\), \(y_2 = 0\)

\[ m = \frac{0 - 0}{9 - 5} = \frac{0}{4} = 0 \]

First, I need to determine the slope of a line that makes an angle of 120 degrees with the positive direction of the x-axis.

I know that the slope (m) of a line is equal to the tangent of the angle it makes with the x-axis. So, m = tan(θ).

Substituting θ = 120 degrees into the formula: m = tan(120°).

The tangent function has specific values at standard angles. Tan(120°) can be expressed as tan(180° - 60°), which is equal to -tan(60°). 

Since tan(60°) is √3, it follows that tan(120°) = -√3.

Therefore, the slope of the line is -√3.
</think>

tan(θ) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Given:
- The angle between the two lines, θ = π/4 (which is 45 degrees).
- The slope of one line, m₁ = 1/4.

I need to find the slope of the other line, m₂.

tan(π/4) = |(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)*m₂)|

Since tan(π/4) is equal to 1, the equation simplifies to:

1 = |(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (m₂)/4)|

To determine the slope of the second line when two lines intersect at an angle of \( \frac{\pi}{4} \) (or \( 45^\circ \)) with one line having a slope (\( m_1 \)) of \( \frac{1}{4} \), we can use the formula that relates the slopes of two intersecting lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

Given:
- \( \theta = \frac{\pi}{4} \)
- \( m_1 = \frac{1}{4} \)

We know that \( \tan\left( \frac{\pi}{4} \right) = 1 \). Substituting the known values into the formula:

\[
1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

This equation can be split into two cases to remove the absolute value:

**Case 1:**
\[
\frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2} = 1
\]
Multiplying both sides by \( 1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2 \):
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2
\]
Rearranging terms:
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2 = 1 + \frac{1}{4}
\]
\[
\frac{3}{4} \cdot m_2 = \frac{5}{4}
\]
Multiplying both sides by \( \frac{4}{3} \):
\[
m_2 = \frac{5}{3}
\]

**Case 2:**
\[
\frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2} = -1
\]
Multiplying both sides by \( 1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2 \):
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 - \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2
\]
Rearranging terms:
\[
m_2 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot m_2 = -1 + \frac{1}{4}
\]
\[
\frac{5}{4} \cdot m_2 = -\frac{3}{4}
\]
Multiplying both sides by \( \frac{4}{5} \):
\[
m_2 = -\frac{3}{5}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The possible slopes of the second line are:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: } \left\{ 5/3,\,-3/5 \right\}}
\]

Next, knowing the slope of the line and a specific point (10, 14) through which another line perpendicular to the first one passes, I can find the equation of this perpendicular line using the point-slope form: \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \). \[ 26 = -3x + 44 \]

To determine the value of \( X \) such that the point \((X, 26)\) lies on a line perpendicular to the one passing through the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line
The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope. Therefore:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)} = -3
\]

### Step 3: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line
Using the point \((10, 14)\) and the slope \( m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -3 \), apply the point-slope form:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
\[
y - 14 = -3(x - 10)
\]

### Step 4: Simplify to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line
\[
y - 14 = -3x + 30
\]
\[
y = -3x + 44
\]

### Step 5: Substitute \( y = 26 \) to Find \( X \)
Substitute \( y = 26 \) into the equation:
\[
26 = -3X + 44
\]
Solving for \( X \):
\[
-18 = -3X
\]
\[
X = 6
\]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{6}

The quadrilateral formed by the points \(A(-6,7)\), \(B(2,9)\), \(C(7,-3)\), and \(D(-2,0)\) arranged in a counter-clockwise order without crossing has an area of \(\frac{157}{2}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Order the Points:** Arrange the points in a specific order (counter-clockwise). For this example, use A, B, C, D.

2. **Apply Shoelace Formula:**
   - Compute S₁ = sum of \(x_i \times y_{i+1}\).
   - Compute S₂ = sum of \(y_i \times x_{i+1}\).

3. **Calculate Area:** The area is half the absolute difference between S₁ and S₂.

Applying these steps:

- **S₁ Calculation:**
  - (-6)×9 + 2×(-3) +7×0 + (-2)×7 = -54 -6 +0 -14 = -74

- **S₂ Calculation:**
  - 7×2 +9×7 + (-3)×(-2) +0×(-6) =14 +63 +6 +0=83

- **Area:** \( \frac{1}{2} | -74 - 83 | = \frac{157}{2} \)

**Answer:** The area of the quadrilateral is \(\boxed{\dfrac{157}{2}}\).

To find the point on the **x-axis** that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Unknown Point
Any point on the x-axis has a y-coordinate of \(0\). Let's denote this unknown point as \((x, 0)\).

### Step 2: Set Up the Distance Equations
The distance between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[ d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \]

Set the distances from \((9, 8)\) to \((x, 0)\) and from \((5, 6)\) to \((x, 0)\) equal:
\[
\sqrt{(x - 9)^2 + (0 - 8)^2} = \sqrt{(x - 5)^2 + (0 - 6)^2}
\]

### Step 3: Square Both Sides to Eliminate the Square Roots
\[
(x - 9)^2 + (-8)^2 = (x - 5)^2 + (-6)^2
\]
\[
(x - 9)^2 + 64 = (x - 5)^2 + 36
\]

Expand both sides:
\[
x^2 - 18x + 81 + 64 = x^2 - 10x + 25 + 36
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
x^2 - 18x + 145 = x^2 - 10x + 61
\]

Subtract \(x^2\) from both sides:
\[
-18x + 145 = -10x + 61
\]

### Step 5: Solve for \(x\)

\[
84 = 8x

Divide by 8:

To determine the **slope** of the line segment connecting the points \( P(2, -2) \) and \( Q(10, 2) \), we can use the formula for slope:

\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the coordinates of both points:**
   - Point \( P \) has coordinates \( (x_1, y_1) = (2, -2) \)
   - Point \( Q \) has coordinates \( (x_2, y_2) = (10, 2) \)

2. **Calculate the change in y:**
   \[
   y_2 - y_1 = 2 - (-2) = 4
   \]

3. **Calculate the change in x:**
   \[
   x_2 - x_1 = 10 - 2 = 8
   \]

4. **Determine the slope by dividing the change in y by the change in x:**
   \[
   \text{Slope} = \frac{4}{8} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5
   \]

To determine if the points (4, 4), (3, 5), and (-1, -1) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, I can use coordinate geometry.

First, calculate the slopes of each side to check for perpendicularity. If any two sides have slopes that are negative reciprocals, their lines are perpendicular, indicating a right angle.

Calculate the slope between (4, 4) and (3, 5):
Slope = (5 - 4)/(3 - 4) = 1/(-1) = -1

Next, find the slope between (3, 5) and (-1, -1):
Slope = (-1 - 5)/(-1 - 3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2

Finally, calculate the slope between (-1, -1) and (4, 4):
Slope = (4 - (-1))/(4 - (-1)) = 5/5 = 1

Now, check if any two slopes are negative reciprocals. Slopes of -1 and 3/2 are not negative reciprocals, nor are 3/2 and 1, or -1 and 1. Therefore, none of the sides are perpendicular.

Since there is no right angle in the triangle formed by these points, they do not form a right-angled triangle.

To determine if the points \((4, 4)\), \((3, 5)\), and \((-1, -1)\) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, we can use coordinate geometry by checking for perpendicular slopes.

### Step 1: Calculate the Slopes of Each Side

The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

1. **Slope between \((4, 4)\) and \((3, 5)\):**
   \[
   m_{AB} = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = \frac{1}{-1} = -1
   \]

2. **Slope between \((3, 5)\) and \((-1, -1)\):**
   \[
   m_{BC} = \frac{-1 - 5}{-1 - 3} = \frac{-6}{-4} = \frac{3}{2}
   \]

3. **Slope between \((-1, -1)\) and \((4, 4)\):**
   \[
   m_{AC} = \frac{4 - (-1)}{4 - (-1)} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
   \]

### Step 2: Check for Perpendicularity

Two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes is \(-1\).

- **Check between \(m_{AB}\) and \(m_{BC}\):**
  \[
  (-1) \times \left(\frac{3}{2}\right) = -\frac{3}{2} \neq -1
  \]

- **Check between \(m_{BC}\) and \(m_{AC}\):**
  \[
  \left(\frac{3}{2}\right) \times (1) = \frac{3}{2} \neq -1
  \]

- **Check between \(m_{AB}\) and \(m_{AC}\):**
  \[
  (-1) \times (1) = -1
  \]

### Conclusion

Since the slopes of sides \(AB\) and \(AC\) have a product of \(-1\), they are perpendicular. This indicates that there is a right angle at point \(A\).

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Yes, the points form a right-angled triangle with the right angle at (4, 4).}}
\]

The problem involves determining the quantity of goods that can be sold at a price of ₹117 per unit, given two data points: when the price is ₹114, 1080 units are sold, and when the price is ₹116, 1320 units are sold. Assuming these points lie on a straight-line supply curve (since higher prices lead to higher quantities supplied), we calculate the slope as:

m = (Q2 - Q1) / (P2 - P1) = (1320 - 1080)/(116 - 114) = 240/2 = 120.

The linear equation is derived using point-slope form:
Q = m(P) + b. Substituting one of the points, say P=114 and Q=1080:
1080 = 120*114 + b → b = -12600.

Thus, Q = 120P - 12600. At P=117:
Q = 120*117 - 12600 = 1440 liters.

**Answer:** The quantity that can be sold at ₹117 per unit is **1,440 liters**.

Let me think about how to calculate the slope. The formula for slope between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). Plugging in the given points: y2 is -9, y1 is 13, x2 is -6, and x1 is 2. So that would be (-9 - 13)/(-6 - 2).

Calculating the numerator first: -9 minus 13 is -22. Then the denominator: -6 minus 2 is -8. So m = (-22)/(-8). Dividing those gives me 22/8, which simplifies to 11/4 or 2.75. Okay, so the slope m is 11/4.

Now that I have the slope, I need to find b, the y-intercept. I can use one of the points to plug into the equation y = mx + b and solve for b. Let's take the point (2, 13) because it might be simpler with smaller numbers.

So plugging in: 13 = (11/4)(2) + b. Calculating (11/4)*2 gives me 22/4, which simplifies to 11/2 or 5.5. So now the equation is 13 = 5.5 + b.

Subtracting 5.5 from both sides: b = 13 - 5.5 = 7.5. Hmm, but it's better to keep it as a fraction maybe? Let me see: 13 is 26/2, and 5.5 is 11/2, so subtracting gives (26 - 11)/2 = 15/2. So b is 15/2.

Now I can write the equation of the line as y = (11/4)x + 15/2. Wait, should I convert this to standard form or something else? The problem didn't specify, so maybe slope-intercept form is fine unless they want it in a different format like Ax + By = C.

= (-66)/4 + 15/2

The equation of the line passing through the points (2, 13) and (-6, -9) is found by first calculating the slope \( m \):

\[
m = \frac{-9 - 13}{-6 - 2} = \frac{-22}{-8} = \frac{11}{4}
\]

Using the point (2, 13) to find the y-intercept \( b \):

\[
13 = \left(\frac{11}{4}\right)(2) + b \\
13 = \frac{22}{4} + b \\
b = 13 - \frac{11}{2} = \frac{26}{2} - \frac{11}{2} = \frac{15}{2}
\]

Thus, the equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:

\[
y = \frac{11}{4}x + \frac{15}{2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the line is \( y = \frac{11}{4}x + \frac{15}{2} \).

The problem requires finding the equation of a line that intercepts both axes at equal distances but in opposite directions and passes through the point (4, 5).

To solve this:

1. **Identify the Intercepts**: Since the line cuts both axes at equal distances with opposite signs, if it intersects the x-axis at (a, 0), it will intersect the y-axis at (0, -a) or vice versa.

2. **Use the Intercept Form of a Line's Equation**:
   The general form is \( \frac{x}{c} + \frac{y}{d} = 1 \).
   Here, since c and d are opposite in sign but equal in magnitude (\( c = a \), \( d = -a \)), substitute these into the equation:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{-a} = 1
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Equation**:
   Combine terms to get:
   \[
   x - y = a
   \]

4. **Determine 'a' Using the Given Point (4,5)**:
   Substitute \( x = 4 \) and \( y = 5 \) into the equation:
   \[
   4 - 5 = a \\
   \Rightarrow a = -1
   \]

5. **Final Equation of the Line**:
   Plug \( a = -1 \) back into the simplified equation to get:
   \[
   x - y = -1
   \]
   
This is the equation of the line that meets the given conditions.

{Final Answer: "x - y = -1"}

First, I need to find the equation of the line that passes through the points (1, 0) and (2, 3). 

I'll start by calculating the slope (m) of the line using the formula:

\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Substituting the given points into the formula:

\[ m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3 \]

Now that I have the slope, I can use the point-slope form of a linear equation to find the equation of the line. Using one of the points, (1, 0), and the slope m = 3:

\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]
\[ y - 0 = 3(x - 1) \]
\[ y = 3x - 3 \]

Therefore, the equation of the line is \( y = 3x - 3 \).
</think>

To find the equation of the line that passes through the points (1, 0) and (2, 3), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope (m) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form
The point-slope form of a line equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using the point (1, 0) and \(m = 3\):
\[
y - 0 = 3(x - 1)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
y = 3x - 3
\]

### Final Answer: 
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 3}
\]

The distance between two parallel lines \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\) is calculated using the formula for the distance between parallel lines:

\[
d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the given values:
- \(A = 3\)
- \(B = -4\)
- \(C_1 = 12\)
- \(C_2 = 7\)

Calculate the numerator: 
\[
|12 - 7| = |5| = 5
\]

Calculate the denominator:
\[
\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

Thus, the distance \(d\) is:
\[
d = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Calculate the slope (m) between points A and B:**
   \( m = (y_2 - y_1)/(x_2 - x_1) = (1 - 5)/(6 - 4) = -2 \)

2. **Determine the equation of line AB using point-slope form with point A(4,5):**
   \( y - 5 = -2(x - 4) \)
   Simplify to get:
   \( y = -2x + 13 \)

3. **Rewrite the equation in standard form:**
   \( 2x + y - 13 = 0 \)

4. **Use the distance formula from point C(3,4) to the line:**
   Distance \( d = |2*3 + 1*4 - 13| / \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2} \)
   Simplify:
   \( d = |-3| / \sqrt{5} = 3/\sqrt{5} \)
   Rationalize the denominator:
   \( d = (3\sqrt{5})/5 \)

**Final Answer:**
The equation of line AB is \( y = -2x + 13 \), and the perpendicular distance from point C to this line is \( \frac{3\sqrt{5}}{5} \).

To find the equation of the perpendicular bisector of the line segment joining (5,6) and (1,4):

1. **Midpoint Calculation**:
   Using the midpoint formula: ((5+1)/2, (6+4)/2) = (3, 5).

2. **Slope of Original Segment**:
   Slope (m) = (4 - 6)/(1 - 5) = (-2)/(-4) = 0.5 or 1/2.

3. **Perpendicular Slope**:
   The negative reciprocal of 1/2 is -2.

4. **Equation Using Point-Slope Form**:
   With midpoint (3,5) and slope -2: y - 5 = -2(x - 3).

5. **Simplifying to Slope-Intercept Form**:
   y - 5 = -2x + 6 → y = -2x + 11.

The problem involves calculating the distances from two points, (A, 5) and (6, 3), to the line defined by the equation \(7x - 9y - 19 = 0\).

For a point \((x_0, y_0)\) and a line \(ax + by + c = 0\), the distance is given by:
\[
d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

**For point (6, 3):**
- Substitute into the formula: \(7(6) - 9(3) -19 = 42 - 27 -19 = -4\)
- Absolute value is 4
- Denominator: \(\sqrt{7^2 + (-9)^2} = \sqrt{49 + 81} = \sqrt{130}\)

Thus, the distance is:
\[
d = \frac{4}{\sqrt{130}} \approx 0.35
\]

**For point (A, 5):**
- Substitute into the formula: \(7A -9(5) -19 = 7A -45 -19 = 7A -64\)
- Absolute value is \(|7A -64|\)

Thus, the distance is:
\[
d = \frac{|7A -64|}{\sqrt{130}}
\]

**Final Answer:**
For point (6, 3): Approximately 0.35 units.

For point (A, 5): The distance depends on the value of A and is expressed as \( \frac{|7A -64|}{\sqrt{130}} \).

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line**:  
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope. Therefore, m₂ = -7.

3. **Use the point-slope form with the x-intercept (5, 0)**:  
   y - 0 = -7(x - 5)  
   Simplifying gives y = -7x + 35.

4. **Convert to standard form**:  
   Bringing all terms to one side yields 7x + y - 35 = 0.

To determine the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line defined by the equation \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), I will follow these steps:

First, I need to rewrite the given equation in the standard form of a linear equation, which is \(Ax + By + C = 0\). Starting with the original equation:
\[24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\]

I'll distribute the coefficients on both sides:
\[24x + 288 = 10y - 40\]

Next, I'll bring all terms to one side to get it into standard form. Subtracting \(10y\) and adding \(40\) gives:
\[24x - 10y + 328 = 0\]

Now that the equation is in standard form, I can use the distance formula from a point \((x_1, y_1)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\), which is:
\[d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}\]

Plugging in the values from our equation and the point, where \(A = 24\), \(B = -10\), \(C = 328\), and \((x_1, y_1) = (-2, 2)\):
\[d = \frac{|24*(-2) + (-10)*2 + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}}\]

Calculating the numerator:
\[24*(-2) = -48\]
\[-10*2 = -20\]
Adding these to \(C\) gives \(-48 -20 + 328 = 260\). Taking the absolute value, it's still 260.

For the denominator:
\[24^2 = 576\]
\[-10^2 = 100\]
Adding these together gives \(576 + 100 = 676\), and taking the square root results in \(\sqrt{676} = 26\).

Finally, dividing the numerator by the denominator:
\[d = \frac{260}{26} = 10\]

Therefore, the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the given line is \(10\) units.

To find the **distance** from the point \( (1, 2) \) to the line given by the equation \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

\[
d = \frac{|Ax + By + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Where:
- The line's equation is \( Ax + By + C = 0 \).
- The point is \( (x, y) \).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients and the point from the given equations:**
   
   - Line equation: \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \)
     - Here, \( A = 4 \), \( B = 7 \), and \( C = 5 \).
   
   - Point: \( (1, 2) \)
     - Here, \( x = 1 \) and \( y = 2 \).

2. **Plug these values into the distance formula:**

\[
d = \frac{|4(1) + 7(2) + 5|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2}} = \frac{|4 + 14 + 5|}{\sqrt{16 + 49}} = \frac{|23|}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

3. **Simplify the expression:**

\[
d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

\[
d = \frac{23 \times \sqrt{65}}{\sqrt{65} \times \sqrt{65}} = \frac{23\sqrt{65}}{65}
\]

\boxed{\text{Final Answer: "}\frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}\text{"}}

\[
\text{Slope } m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

- Point \( A(x_1, y_1) = (5, 0) \)

   \[

   \]

To find the value of \( x \) for which the line passing through the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\) also passes through the point \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Line
The slope (\( m \)) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\):
\[
m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

### Step 2: Find the Equation of the Line
Using the point-slope form of a line:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substitute \( m = \frac{1}{3} \) and the point \((0, 8)\):
\[
y - 8 = \frac{1}{3}(x - 0) \\
y = \frac{1}{3}x + 8
\]

### Step 3: Find \( x \) When \( y = 26 \)
Substitute \( y = 26 \) into the equation:
\[
26 = \frac{1}{3}x + 8 \\
\]
Solve for \( x \):
\[
26 - 8 = \frac{1}{3}x \\
18 = \frac{1}{3}x \\
x = 18 \times 3 \\
x = 54
\]

### Final Answer
The value of \( x \) is:

\boxed{54}

To determine the **area** of the quadrilateral with vertices at \((-6, 7)\), \((2, 9)\), \((7, -3)\), and \((-2, 0)\), we can use the **shoelace formula**. This method allows us to calculate the area based on the coordinates of the vertices.

### Steps:

1. **List the Coordinates in Order:**
   ```
   A(-6, 7), B(2, 9), C(7, -3), D(-2, 0)
   ```

2. **Apply the Shoelace Formula:**
   
   The formula is given by:
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \left| (x_1y_2 + x_2y_3 + x_3y_4 + x_4y_1) - (y_1x_2 + y_2x_3 + y_3x_4 + y_4x_1) \right|
   \]
   
   Plugging in the coordinates:
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \left| (-6 \times 9 + 2 \times -3 + 7 \times 0 + -2 \times 7) - (7 \times 2 + 9 \times 7 + -3 \times -2 + 0 \times -6) \right|
   \]
   
3. **Calculate the Values:**
   
   Compute each multiplication and sum:
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left| (-54 - 6 + 0 - 14) - (14 + 63 + 6 + 0) \right|
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left| (-74) - (83) \right|
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left| -157 \right|
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \times 157
   \]
   
4. **Finalize the Calculation:**
   
   Multiply by \(\frac{1}{2}\):
   \[
   = 78.5
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{\text{Area} = 78.5}
\]

So, let's label the points for clarity. Let me call them A(4, 4), B(3, 5), and C(-1, -1). Now, I need to find the slopes of AB, BC, and AC because if any two sides are perpendicular, their slopes should be negative reciprocals.

Calculating slope AB: The formula for slope between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). So for AB, it's (5 - 4)/(3 - 4) = 1/(-1) = -1. Okay, so the slope of AB is -1.

Next, slope BC: Using points B(3,5) and C(-1,-1), the slope would be (-1 -5)/(-1 -3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2. So slope BC is 3/2.

Lastly, slope AC: Between A(4,4) and C(-1,-1), it's (-1 -4)/(-1 -4) = (-5)/(-5) = 1. Therefore, slope AC is 1.

Now, checking for perpendicularity between any two sides:

- AB (slope -1) and BC (slope 3/2): Multiplying them gives (-1)*(3/2) = -3/2 ≠ -1.
- AB (-1) and AC (1): Multiplying gives (-1)*1 = -1, which is the condition for perpendicularity. So wait, that means sides AB and AC are perpendicular.

But let me double-check because sometimes I might mix up points or calculations. Let me re-calculate each slope:

Slope AB: (5-4)/(3-4) = 1/-1 = -1 ✔️

Slope BC: (-1-5)/(-1-3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2 ✔️

Slope AC: (-1-4)/(-1-4) = (-5)/(-5)=1 ✔️

So, slopes are correct. Now, checking the products:

AB * BC: -1*(3/2) = -3/2 (not equal to -1)
AB * AC: -1*1= -1 (yes, this is perpendicular)
BC * AC: 3/2*1 = 3/2 (not equal to -1)

Therefore, sides AB and AC are perpendicular. Hence, the triangle formed by these three points is a right-angled triangle with the right angle at point A.

Alternatively, I could have also checked using vectors or dot product because if two vectors are perpendicular, their dot product is zero. Let me try that approach quickly to verify.

Vector AB would be from A(4,4) to B(3,5), which is (3-4, 5-4)=(-1,1). Vector AC would be from A(4,4) to C(-1,-1), which is (-1-4, -1-4)=(-5,-5).

Dot product of AB and AC vectors: (-1)*(-5) + (1)*(-5) = 5 -5=0. Since the dot product is zero, they are indeed perpendicular.

So both methods confirm that point A is the right angle. Therefore, the triangle is right-angled at A.

To determine if the given points \((-2, -1)\), \((4, 0)\), \((3, 3)\), and \((-3, 2)\) form a parallelogram using the midpoint formula, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Pairs of Opposite Points
Assume the points are connected in pairs:
- Pair 1: \((-2, -1)\) and \((4, 0)\)
- Pair 2: \((3, 3)\) and \((-3, 2)\)

### Step 2: Calculate Midpoints Using the Midpoint Formula
The midpoint formula is:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]

#### Midpoint of Pair 1:
\[
M_1 = \left( \frac{-2 + 4}{2}, \frac{-1 + 0}{2} \right) = \left( \frac{2}{2}, \frac{-1}{2} \right) = (1, -0.5)
\]

#### Midpoint of Pair 2:
\[
M_2 = \left( \frac{3 + (-3)}{2}, \frac{3 + 2}{2} \right) = \left( \frac{0}{2}, \frac{5}{2} \right) = (0, 2.5)
\]

### Step 3: Compare the Midpoints
Since \(M_1\) is \((1, -0.5)\) and \(M_2\) is \((0, 2.5)\), the midpoints are **not** the same.

### Conclusion
The given points do not form a parallelogram because the diagonals (the line segments connecting opposite vertices) do not have the same midpoint.

To solve the problem of finding two lines passing through the point \((-1, m)\) with slopes \(\frac{2}{1 + m}\) and \(-\frac{1 + m}{2}\), we can follow these steps:

1. **Write the equations of the lines**:
   - For the first line with slope \(\frac{2}{1 + m}\):
     \[
     y - m = \frac{2}{1 + m}(x + 1)
     \]
   - For the second line with slope \(-\frac{1 + m}{2}\):
     \[
     y - m = -\frac{1 + m}{2}(x + 1)
     \]

2. **Check if the lines are perpendicular**:
   - Calculate the product of their slopes:
     \[
     \left(\frac{2}{1 + m}\right) \times \left(-\frac{1 + m}{2}\right) = -1
     \]
   - The product is \(-1\) as long as \(1 + m \neq 0\), which means \(m \neq -1\).

Thus, the lines are perpendicular for all real numbers \(m\) except \(m = -1\).

### Final Answer
The value of \( m \) can be any real number except \(-1\), so the solution is \(\boxed{m \neq -1}\).

**Solution:**

We need to find the equation of the line that passes through the points \((3, 1)\) and \((5, 7)\).

1. **Find the Slope (\(m\))**:
   
   The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:
   \[
   m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form**:
   
   The point-slope form of a line equation is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the point \((3, 1)\) and the slope \(m = 3\):
   \[
   y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form**:
   
   Distribute the slope on the right side:
   \[
   y - 1 = 3x - 9
   \]
   
   Add \(1\) to both sides to solve for \(y\):
   \[
   y = 3x - 8
   \]

4. **Final Equation**:
   
   The equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:
   \[
   \boxed{y = 3x - 8}
   \]

First, I need to establish a linear relationship between price and quantity demanded based on the given data points.

We have two sets of data:
- At a price of ₹114 per liter, 1080 liters are sold daily.
- At a price of ₹1320 per liter, 116 liters are sold daily.

I'll denote the price as P and the quantity demanded as Q. The linear equation can be expressed as:
Q = m * P + b

Using the two data points, I'll set up equations to solve for the slope (m) and the y-intercept (b).

Once I have the equation of the line, I can plug in the target price of ₹117 per liter to find out how many liters would be sold daily at that price.

This approach allows me to determine the quantity demanded at any given price within the linear demand curve.
</think>

To determine how many liters of milk a cooperative can sell at a target price, we'll establish a **linear demand function** based on the given data and then use it to find the quantity sold at ₹117 per liter.

### Step 1: Define Variables
- Let \( P \) be the price per liter (₹).
- Let \( Q \) be the quantity demanded in liters.

### Step 2: Identify Known Points
We have two data points:
1. At \( P = 114 \), \( Q = 1080 \)
2. At \( P = 1320 \), \( Q = 116 \)

### Step 3: Calculate the Slope (\( m \)) of the Demand Curve
The slope formula is:
\[
m = \frac{Q_2 - Q_1}{P_2 - P_1}
\]
Substituting the known values:
\[
m = \frac{116 - 1080}{1320 - 114} = \frac{-964}{1206} \approx -0.799
\]

### Step 4: Determine the Y-Intercept (\( b \)) of the Demand Curve
Using one of the points (e.g., \( P = 1320 \), \( Q = 116 \)):
\[
Q = mP + b \\
116 = (-0.799)(1320) + b \\
116 \approx -1054.68 + b \\
b \approx 1170.68
\]

### Step 5: Formulate the Demand Equation
\[
Q = -0.799P + 1170.68
\]

### Step 6: Calculate Quantity Demanded at \( P = 117 \)
Substitute \( P = 117 \) into the equation:
\[
Q = -0.799(117) + 1170.68 \\

To find the equation of the line making an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians with the positive x-axis and passing through the point (0, 2), we start by determining the slope:

1. **Slope Calculation**:
   - The tangent of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) is calculated as follows: 
     \[
     \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = \tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}
     \]
   - Thus, the slope (m) of the line is \( -\sqrt{3} \).

2. **Equation of the Line**:
   - Using the point-slope form with the y-intercept (0, 2), we substitute into the equation \( y = mx + b \):
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
     \]

3. **Parallel Line**:
   - A line parallel to the first will have the same slope (\( -\sqrt{3} \)) but intersect the y-axis at a different point.
   - To find the equation of this parallel line, we need to determine its y-intercept (b). If it intersects the y-axis 2 units below the origin, the new intercept is (0, -2).

4. **Equation of the Parallel Line**:
   - Substituting \( m = -\sqrt{3} \) and \( b = -2 \) into \( y = mx + b \):
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2
     \]

### Final Answer

- **Equation of the original line**: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \)
- **Equation of the parallel line**: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \)

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Identify the Points:**
   - Point A: (4, 7)
   - Point B: (-5, 8)

2. **Calculate the Slope (m) between the two points:**
   
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = \frac{-1}{9}
   \]

3. **Use Point A to Find the Y-intercept (b):**
   
   Substitute \( m = \frac{-1}{9} \) and point A's coordinates into the equation of a line:
   
   \[
   y = mx + b
   \]
   
   Plugging in values:
   
   \[
   7 = \left(\frac{-1}{9}\right)(4) + b
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   7 = \frac{-4}{9} + b \\
   b = 7 + \frac{4}{9} \\
   b = \frac{63}{9} + \frac{4}{9} = \frac{67}{9}
   \]

4. **Write the Equation of the Line:**
   
   Substitute \( m = \frac{-1}{9} \) and \( b = \frac{67}{9} \) into the slope-intercept form:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{-1}{9}x + \frac{67}{9}
   \]
   
   However, there seems to be a calculation error in finding \( b \). Let's correct it:

   **Correct Calculation:**
   
   Using point A (4, 7):
   
   \[
   7 = \left(\frac{-1}{9}\right)(4) + b \\
   7 = \frac{-4}{9} + b \\
   b = 7 + \frac{4}{9} = \frac{63}{9} + \frac{4}{9} = \frac{67}{9}
   \]
   
   Wait, this still results in \( b = \frac{67}{9} \). However, using point B (-5, 8):
   
   Substitute into the equation:
   
   \[
   8 = \left(\frac{-1}{9}\right)(-5) + b \\
   8 = \frac{5}{9} + b \\
   b = 8 - \frac{5}{9} = \frac{72}{9} - \frac{5}{9} = \frac{67}{9}
   \]
   
   Therefore, the correct equation is:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{-1}{9}x + \frac{67}{9}
   \]

It also intersects the positive x-axis 5 units to the right of the origin at (5, 0).

Next, I use the point-slope form with one of the points to determine the equation. Using the point (0, -4), the equation becomes y = (4/5)x - 4.

Finally, I write the equation in standard form by rearranging it to 4x - 5y - 20 = 0.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given that a line intersects the negative \( y \)-axis at \( (0, -4) \) and the positive \( x \)-axis at \( (5, 0) \).

1. **Find the slope (\( m \)) of the line:**
   
   The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) = (0, -4) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) = (5, 0) \) is calculated as:
   
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - (-4)}{5 - 0} = \frac{4}{5}
   \]

2. **Use the slope-intercept form to find the equation:**
   
   The slope-intercept form of a line is:
   
   \[
   y = mx + b
   \]
   
   We already have \( m = \frac{4}{5} \) and we know that when \( x = 0 \), \( y = -4 \). This point gives us the \( y\)-intercept (\( b \)):
   
   \[
   -4 = \frac{4}{5}(0) + b \implies b = -4
   \]
   
   Therefore, the equation of the line is:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{4}{5}x - 4
   \]

3. **Convert to standard form:**
   
   To write the equation in standard form (\( Ax + By + C = 0 \)), rearrange the slope-intercept form:
   
   \[
   \frac{4}{5}x - y - 4 = 0
   \]
   
   Multiply through by 5 to eliminate the fraction:
   
   \[
   4x - 5y - 20 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the line is:

\[
\boxed{4x - 5y - 20 = 0}
\]

   - A = 5
   - B = -6
   - C = -28

Distance = |a*x₀ + b*y₀ + c| / sqrt(a² + b²)

Substituting the values for the second line (3x -4y +7=0), the point is (0, 3). Calculating the numerator: |3*0 -4*3 +7| = |-12 +7| = |-5| = 5.

To find the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[
3x - 4y + 12 = 0
\]
\[
3x - 4y + 7 = 0
\]

we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line. Since both lines are identical in their coefficients except for the constant term, they are parallel.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify Coefficients:**
   - For both lines, \(a = 3\), \(b = -4\).

2. **Choose a Point on One Line:**
   - Let's choose the point \((0, 3)\) from the first line when \(x = 0\):
     \[
     3(0) - 4y + 12 = 0 \Rightarrow y = 3
     \]

3. **Apply the Distance Formula:**
   - The formula for distance (\(d\)) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(ax + by + c = 0\) is:
     \[
     d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
     \]
   - Substituting \(a = 3\), \(b = -4\), and the point \((0, 3)\) into the second line's equation (\(c = 7\)):
     \[
     d = \frac{|3(0) + (-4)(3) + 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{|-5|}{5} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**

The distance between the two parallel lines is:

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

The formula for the distance \( d \) from a point \( (x_1, y_1) \) to the line \( Ax + By + C = 0 \) is:

\[
d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

For the given point \( (1, 5) \) and the line \( 3x - 4y - 16 = 0 \), we have:
- \( A = 3 \)
- \( B = -4 \)
- \( C = -16 \)
- \( x_1 = 1 \)
- \( y_1 = 5 \)

Plugging these into the formula:

\[
d = \frac{|3(1) + (-4)(5) - 16|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{|3 - 20 - 16|}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{|-33|}{5} = \frac{33}{5} = 6.6
\]

The distance is \( 6.6 \) units.

To find the equation of the perpendicular bisector for the line segment connecting the points \((5, 6)\) and \((1, 4)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Midpoint**:
   The midpoint \(M\) is calculated as:
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the Slope of the Original Line Segment**:
   The slope \(m\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

3. **Determine the Perpendicular Bisector's Slope**:
   The slope of the perpendicular bisector \(m_{\perp}\) is the negative reciprocal of \(m\):
   \[
   m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{m} = -2
   \]

4. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation**:
   Using the midpoint \((3, 5)\) and slope \(-2\), apply the point-slope formula \(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\):
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 3)

5. **Simplify to Standard Form**:

   \[
   y - 5 = -2x + 6 \\
   2x + y = 11 \\
   2x + y - 11 = 0
   \]

\boxed{2x + y - 11 = 0}

To determine the value of \( h \) for which the angle between the lines \( y = x + h \) and \( y = -x + 2h \) is obtuse, we start by analyzing their slopes. The first line has a slope of 1, and the second line has a slope of -1.

The formula to find the tangent of the angle \( \theta \) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Substituting the given slopes \( m_1 = 1 \) and \( m_2 = -1 \):
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-1 - 1}{1 + (1)(-1)} \right| = \left| \frac{-2}{0} \right|
\]

Since the denominator is zero, this results in an undefined value for \( \tan(\theta) \), indicating that \( \theta \) is 90 degrees. An angle of 90 degrees is a right angle, not an obtuse angle (which requires an angle greater than 90 degrees).

Thus, regardless of the value of \( h \), the angle between the lines remains at 90 degrees. Therefore, there is no positive integer \( h \) that makes the angle between the two lines obtuse.

\[
\boxed{\emptyset}
\]

To find the angle between the lines given by the equations √3 x + y = 1 and x + √3 y = 1:

1. **Find the slopes of both lines:**
   - For √3 x + y = 1:
     Solve for y: y = -√3 x + 1 → Slope (m₁) = -√3.
   - For x + √3 y = 1:
     Solve for y: y = (-1/√3)x + (1/√3) → Slope (m₂) = -1/√3.

2. **Use the formula to find the tangent of the angle between them:**
   tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

   Plugging in m₁ and m₂:
   tanθ = |(-1/√3 - (-√3))/(1 + (-√3)*(-1/√3))|
   
   Simplify numerator: (-1/√3 + √3) = (2)/√3
   Denominator: 1 + (√3*(1/√3)) = 2
   
   So, tanθ = |(2/√3)/2| = |1/√3|

3. **Determine the angle θ:**
   Since tanθ = 1/√3, which corresponds to θ = 30°.

**Final Answer:** The angle between the two lines is 30 degrees.

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through \((-4, 5)\), we use the fact that parallel lines have identical coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\). Thus, the new line will be of the form \(5x - 6y + C = 0\). Substituting the point into this equation:

\[
5(-4) - 6(5) + C = 0 \\
-20 - 30 + C = 0 \\
C = 50
\]

Hence, the equation is \(5x - 6y + 50 = 0\).

Final Answer: \boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}

To determine the distance between two parallel lines given by their equations, I first verify that they are indeed parallel. This is done by checking if their slopes are equal.

The general form of a line is ax + by + c = 0, and its slope is -a/b. For both equations:
- The slope of 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 is -19/12.
- The slope of 38x + 24y + 70 = 0 simplifies to the same value, confirming they are parallel.

Next, I use the distance formula between two parallel lines:
Distance (d) = |c₁ - c₂| / √(a² + b²)

Here, a = 19, b = 12. The constants c₁ and c₂ need to be adjusted based on their positions in the equations:
- From 19x + 12y - 38 = 0, c₁ is -38.
- From 38x + 24y + 70 = 0, I divide the entire equation by 2 to get 19x + 12y + 35 = 0, making c₂ = 35.

Calculating the absolute difference between the constants: |-38 - 35| = 73.

Finally, compute the distance:
d = |73| / √(19² + 12²) = 73 / √(361 + 144) = 73 / √505 ≈ 1.03

This gives the distance between the two parallel lines.

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 is calculated using the formula for distance from a point to a line.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the coefficients and point:**
   - Line equation: 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 ⇒ A = 4, B = 7, C = 5
   - Point: (x₁, y₁) = (1, 2)

2. **Apply the distance formula:**
   \[
   d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]

3. **Calculate numerator:**
   \[
   |4*1 + 7*2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23|
   \]

4. **Calculate denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}
   \]

5. **Simplify the distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} 
   \]
   Rationalizing the denominator:
   \[
   d = \frac{23 \sqrt{65}}{65}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line is \( \frac{23\sqrt{65}}{65} \).

To find the perpendicular distance from the point \( P(4, 1) \) to the line \( 4x - y = 0 \), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

First, rewrite the given equation in standard form:
\[ 4x - y = 0 \]
This can be written as:
\[ 4x - y + 0 = 0 \]
So, \( A = 4 \), \( B = -1 \), and \( C = 0 \).

The point is \( (x_0, y_0) = (4, 1) \).

Now, plug these values into the distance formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|4(4) + (-1)(1) + 0|}{\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{|16 - 1|}{\sqrt{16 + 1}} = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}}
\]

Hold on, actually, perhaps it's better to present the equations as I did before because they are explicit angle bisectors. However, another approach is to note that when the distances from P(x,y) to L₁ and L₂ are equal, the equation |distance to L₁| = |distance to L₂|. Squaring both sides, so:

\[
\frac{|x + \sqrt{37} y - 5|^2}{(1^2 + (\sqrt{37})^2)} = \frac{|-\sqrt{10} x - y -4|^2}{((-\sqrt{10})^2 + (-1)^2)}
\]

So, computing denominators:

Denominator for L₁: 1 + 37=38.

Denominator for L₂: 10 +1=11.

Thus,

\[
\frac{(x + \sqrt{37} y -5)^2}{38} = \frac{(-\sqrt{10}x - y -4)^2}{11}
\]

Cross-multiplying:

11(x + √37 y -5)^2 = 38(√10 x + y +4)^2

This would give a combined equation of both angle bisectors. However, the problem asks for all points where distance to L₁ equals distance to L₂, so technically this single equation represents both bisectors.

But earlier in my thought process I found two specific equations (Case 1 and Case 2), each corresponding to one of these two bisectors. So perhaps it's better to present them together as a single combined equation by setting the expressions equal with absolute values:

The equality |distance to L₁| = |distance to L₂|

Which translates into:

\[
\frac{|x + \sqrt{37} y -5|}{\sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{37})^2}} = \frac{|-\sqrt{10} x - y -4|}{\sqrt{(\sqrt{10})^2 + (-1)^2}}
\]

Simplify the denominators:

Denominator for L₁: sqrt(1+37)=sqrt(38).

Denominator for L₂: sqrt(10+1)=sqrt(11).

To find the equation representing all points equidistant from two fixed points (A and B), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Coordinates**: Let A be \((x_1, y_1)\) and B be \((x_2, y_2)\).

2. **Find the Midpoint**: The midpoint M between A and B is given by:
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
   \]

3. **Calculate the Slope of AB**:
   \[
   m_{AB} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
4. **Determine the Perpendicular Bisector's Slope**: The slope of the perpendicular bisector is the negative reciprocal of \(m_{AB}\):
   \[
   m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{m_{AB}}
   \]

5. **Form the Equation Using Point-Slope Form**:
   \[
   y - M_y = m_{\text{perp}}(x - M_x)
   \]
   
6. **Simplify the Equation**: Rearrange to standard form if needed.

This equation represents all points equidistant from A and B, forming a line perpendicular to AB and passing through its midpoint.

For example, if A is (1, 2) and B is (3, 4):
- Midpoint M = (2, 3)
- Slope of AB: \(m_{AB} = \frac{4 - 2}{3 - 1} = 1\)
- Perpendicular slope: \(m_{\text{perp}} = -1\)
- Equation: \(y - 3 = -1(x - 2)\) → \(y = -x + 5\) or \(x + y - 5 = 0\)

Answer: \boxed{x + y - 5 = 0}

First, I need to determine the coordinates of all four vertices of the quadrilateral given as (-6, 7), (2, 9), (7, -3), and (-2, 0).

Next, to calculate the area of an irregular quadrilateral when the coordinates of its vertices are known, one effective method is to use the shoelace formula. This involves listing the coordinates in order either clockwise or counterclockwise and then applying the formula.

I'll list the points in a specific order: (-6, 7), (2, 9), (7, -3), (-2, 0). After that, I'll repeat the first point at the end to complete the cycle for the shoelace calculation.

Applying the formula:
Area = (1/2) |(x₁y₂ + x₂y₃ + x₃y₄ + x₄y₁) - (y₁x₂ + y₂x₃ + y₃x₄ + y₄x₁)|

Plugging in the values:
= (1/2) |[(-6×9) + (2×-3) + (7×0) + (-2×7)] - [(7×2) + (9×7) + (-3×-2) + (0×-6)]|

**Solution:**

To find the area of the quadrilateral with vertices at \((-6, 7)\), \((2, 9)\), \((7, -3)\), and \((-2, 0)\), we can use the **shoelace formula**. This method is particularly useful when the coordinates of all four vertices are known.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **List the Coordinates in Order:**
   Ensure that the points are listed either clockwise or counterclockwise and repeat the first point at the end to complete the cycle.
   
   | Point  | \(x\) | \(y\) |
   |--------|-------|-------|
   | A      | -6    | 7     |
   | B      | 2     | 9     |
   | C      | 7     | -3    |
   | D      | -2    | 0     |
   | A      | -6    | 7     |

2. **Apply the Shoelace Formula:**
   
   The formula for the area of a polygon given its vertices \((x_1, y_1)\), \((x_2, y_2)\), ..., \((x_n, y_n)\) is:
   
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \left| \sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i y_{i+1} - x_{i+1} y_i) \right|
   \]
   
   Where \(x_{n+1} = x_1\) and \(y_{n+1} = y_1\).

3. **Compute the Terms:**
   
   Create two sums:
   
   - Sum of \(x_i y_{i+1}\):
     \[
     (-6 \times 9) + (2 \times -3) + (7 \times 0) + (-2 \times 7) = -54 - 6 + 0 - 14 = -74
     \]
   
   - Sum of \(y_i x_{i+1}\):
     \[
     (7 \times 2) + (9 \times 7) + (-3 \times -2) + (0 \times -6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
     \]
   
   Calculate the difference:
   
   \[
   |-74 - 83| = |-157| = 157
   \]

4. **Calculate the Area:**
   
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times 157 = 78.5
   \]
   
   Since area is always positive, we take the absolute value.

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: "78.5"}
\]

To find the slope of a line that makes a 60-degree angle with the positive y-axis, I start by determining the corresponding angle with respect to the positive x-axis.

Since the total angle around a point is 360 degrees and angles on either side sum up to 90 degrees in the first quadrant, if a line makes a 60-degree angle with the positive y-axis, it would make an angle of 30 degrees (90 - 60) with the positive x-axis.

Next, I calculate the slope using the tangent function. The slope m is equal to the tangent of this 30-degree angle.

I recall that tan(30°) is √3/3 or approximately 0.5774.

Therefore, the slope of the line is √3 divided by 3.
</think>

To determine the slope of a line that makes an angle of **60 degrees** with the positive y-axis, follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Relationship Between Angles:**
   
   - The total angle around the origin is **360 degrees**.
   - On either side of the y-axis (positive and negative), angles sum up to **90 degrees**.

2. **Calculate the Corresponding Angle with the x-axis:**

   \[
   90^\circ - 60^\circ = 30^\circ
   \]

   So, the angle made with the positive x-axis is **30 degrees**.

3. **Find the Slope Using the Tangent Function:**

   The slope \( m \) of a line is given by:

   \[
   m = \tan(\theta)
   \]

   where \( \theta \) is the angle made with the positive x-axis.

4. **Substitute the Known Angle:**

   \[
   m = \tan(30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
   \]

   Alternatively, in decimal form:

   \[
   m \approx 0.5774
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The slope of the line is **\(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\)** or approximately **0.5774**.

I need to determine the angle that the line connecting the points (5,1) and (6,0) makes with the x-axis.

First, I'll calculate the slope of the line using the formula:

slope = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)

Plugging in the given points:

slope = (0 - 1)/(6 - 5) = (-1)/1 = -1

With a slope of -1, I know that the angle θ with respect to the positive x-axis can be found using the arctangent function:

tan(θ) = slope
tan(θ) = -1

The arctangent of -1 is -45 degrees. However, since angles are typically measured in the counterclockwise direction from the positive x-axis and negative angles represent a clockwise rotation, I'll convert this to a positive angle by adding 180 degrees:

θ = 180° + (-45°) = 135°

Thus, the angle between the line and the x-axis is 135 degrees.
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given two points, (5,1) and (6,0), and we need to find the angle that the line connecting these points makes with the positive direction of the x-axis.

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope \( m \) of a line passing through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
m = \frac{0 - 1}{6 - 5} = \frac{-1}{1} = -1
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Angle with the X-axis
The angle \( \theta \) that a line makes with the positive direction of the x-axis can be found using:
\[
\tan(\theta) = m
\]
Given \( m = -1 \):
\[
\tan(\theta) = -1
\]
The arctangent of \(-1\) is \(-45^\circ\). However, since angles are typically measured in the counterclockwise direction from the positive x-axis and negative angles represent a clockwise rotation, we convert this to a positive angle by adding \( 180^\circ \):
\[
\theta = -45^\circ + 180^\circ = 135^\circ
\]

### Final Answer
The angle between the line and the x-axis is:
\[
\boxed{135^\circ}
\]

The problem involves finding the slopes of two lines where one slope is double the other, and the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3.

Given:
- Let \( m_1 \) be the slope of the first line, \( m_2 \) be the slope of the second line.
- \( m_1 = 2m_2 \).
- The tangent of the angle between the lines is given by \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \).

Using the formula for the angle between two lines:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
Substituting \( m_1 = 2m_2 \):
\[
\frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{m_2 - 2m_2}{1 + (2m_2)m_2} \right| = \left| \frac{-m_2}{1 + 2m_2^2} \right|
\]
Simplifying:
\[
\frac{1}{3} = \frac{m_2}{1 + 2m_2^2}
\]
Cross-multiplying and solving the quadratic equation:
\[
1 + 2m_2^2 = 3m_2 \\
2m_2^2 - 3m_2 + 1 = 0
\]
Using the quadratic formula:
\[
m_2 = \frac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 8}}{4} = \frac{3 \pm 1}{4}
\]
Thus, \( m_2 = 1 \) or \( m_2 = 0.5 \).

Corresponding to these values:
- If \( m_2 = 1 \), then \( m_1 = 2 \times 1 = 2 \).
- If \( m_2 = 0.5 \), then \( m_1 = 2 \times 0.5 = 1 \).

Therefore, the slopes of the two lines are either (2 and 1) or (1 and 0.5).

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{2} \text{ and } \boxed{1}
\]

Sure! Let's solve the problem step-by-step.

To determine the equations of the lines based on the given conditions:

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Determine the Slope:**
   - The angle with respect to the positive x-axis is \( \theta = \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians, which is 120 degrees.
   - The slope (\( m \)) of the line is given by \( \tan(\theta) \).
   - Since \( \tan(60^\circ) = \sqrt{3} \), and knowing that tangent is negative in the second quadrant, we have:
     \[
     m = \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = -\sqrt{3}
     \]

2. **Equation of the Line Passing Through (0, 2):**
   - Using the slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \), where the point (0, 2) is the y-intercept.
   - Substituting the values:
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
     \]

3. **Equations of Parallel Lines Crossing the Y-Axis at \( y = 1 \) and \( y = 3 \):**
   - Since parallel lines have the same slope, both will have a slope of \( -\sqrt{3} \).
   - For the line crossing at (0, 1):
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
     \]
   - For the line crossing at (0, 3):
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 3
     \]

First, I need to find the equation of a line that is perpendicular to the line passing through the points (4, 7) and (-5, 8). 

To do this, I'll start by calculating the slope of the given line using the formula:

m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)

Substituting the values:
m = (8 - 7)/(-5 - 4) = 1/-9 = -1/9

The slope of the perpendicular line is the negative reciprocal of this, which is 9.

Now that I have the slope of the perpendicular line, I can use one of the given points to find the y-intercept (b). Let's use the point (4,7):

y = mx + b
7 = 9*4 + b
7 = 36 + b
b = 7 - 36
b = -29

Finally, I'll write the equation of the perpendicular line in slope-intercept form:
y = 9x - 29
</think>

Let's determine the equation of the line that is perpendicular to the line passing through the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\), and passes through one of these points.

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line
The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope. Therefore:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\left(\frac{-1}{\frac{1}{9}}\right) = 9
\]

### Step 3: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation
We can use one of the given points, say \((4, 7)\), and the perpendicular slope \(m_{\text{perpendicular}} = 9\) to write the equation in point-slope form:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
y - 7 = 9(x - 4)
\]

### Step 4: Convert to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 7 = 9x - 36 \\
y = 9x - 36 + 7 \\
y = 9x - 29
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 9x - 29}
\]

The given points P(2,1), Q(-2,3), and R(4,5) are not collinear as the slopes between pairs of points are different.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Check for Collinearity:**
   - Calculate slope between P and Q:
     \[
     m_{PQ} = \frac{3 - 1}{-2 - 2} = \frac{2}{-4} = -0.5
     \]
   - Calculate slope between Q and R:
     \[
     m_{QR} = \frac{5 - 3}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
     \]
   Since \(m_{PQ} \neq m_{QR}\), the points are not collinear.

**Answer:**
The points P, Q, and R do not lie on a single straight line. Therefore, it is impossible to determine a single equation of a line that passes through all three points because they are not collinear.

We need to find the maximum value of \( x^2 + y^2 \) under the constraints given by the inequalities:

\[ (x+4)^2 + y^2 \leq 25 \]
and
\[ |x| \leq \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, \quad |y| \leq \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \]

The first inequality represents a circle centered at \((-4, 0)\) with radius 5. The second set of inequalities defines a square centered at the origin with side length \(\sqrt{2}\), extending from \(-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\) to \(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\) on both axes.

We need to check if all points within this square are inside the circle. The farthest point from \((-4, 0)\) in the square is at \(\left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)\). Calculating its distance squared:

\[ \left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} + 4 \right)^2 + \left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)^2 = \left( 4.707 \right)^2 + 0.5 \approx 22.136 + 0.5 = 22.636 \]

Since this is less than \(25\), all points of the square are within the circle.

The maximum value of \( x^2 + y^2 \) occurs at the corners of the square where both \( |x| \) and \( |y| \) are maximized. At these points, each squared term is \(\left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)^2 = 0.5\), so their sum is:

\[ x^2 + y^2 = 0.5 + 0.5 = 1 \]

Thus, the maximum value of \( x^2 + y^2 \) in this region is \(\boxed{1}\).

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y -28 = 0 can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Coefficients and Point:**
   The given line is in the form Ax + By + C = 0, where A = 5, B = -6, and C = -28.
   The point is (x₀, y₀) = (5, -7).

2. **Apply the Distance Formula:**
   The formula to find the distance (d) from the point (x₀, y₀) to the line Ax + By + C = 0 is:
   \[
   d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]

3. **Plug in the Values:**
   Substitute A, B, C, and the coordinates into the formula:
   \[
   d = \frac{|5*5 + (-6)*(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]

4. **Calculate the Numerator:**
   \[
   |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39
   \]

5. **Calculate the Denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

6. **Find the Distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]
   This can be rationalized as:
   \[
   d = \frac{39 \sqrt{61}}{61}
   \]

7. **Approximate Value (Optional):**
   If an approximate decimal value is needed, divide 39 by √61 (approximately 7.81):
   \[
   d ≈ \frac{39}{7.81} ≈ 4.996
   \]
   Rounded to one decimal place: 5.0.

**Final Answer:**  
The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line is approximately 5.0 units when expressed numerically.

To solve the given system involving the expressions \(3x\) and some other terms (not explicitly detailed here but inferred from the context), we need to analyze the range of values for which a particular expression holds.

1. We start by considering the inequalities derived from the problem:
   - From one inequality, we get a lower bound on \(x\): \((40 - c)/17\).
   - From another inequality, we get an upper bound on \(x\): \((60 - c)/3\).

2. To ensure that there exists an \(x\) satisfying both bounds, the lower bound must be less than or equal to the upper bound:
   \[
   \frac{40 - c}{17} \leq \frac{60 - c}{3}
   \]

3. Solving this inequality for \(c\):
   \[
   3(40 - c) \leq 17(60 - c)
   \]
   Simplifying both sides:
   \[
   120 - 3c \leq 1020 - 17c
   \]
   Bringing like terms together:
   \[
   -3c + 17c \leq 1020 - 120
   \]
   \[
   14c \leq 900
   \]
   Dividing both sides by 14:
   \[
   c \leq \frac{900}{14} = \frac{450}{7}
   \]

Thus, the value of \(c\) can be any real number less than or equal to \(\frac{450}{7}\).

The solution is \boxed{\left(-\infty, \dfrac{450}{7}\right]}.

To find the distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0, I'll use the formula for the distance from a point to a line. 

First, I identify the coefficients A, B, and C from the given equation of the line. Here, A is 4, B is 7, and C is 5.

Next, I substitute these values along with the coordinates of the point (x₀ = 1 and y₀ = 2) into the distance formula:

Distance = |A x₀ + B y₀ + C| / √(A² + B²)

Plugging in the numbers:

Numerator: |4*1 + 7*2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = 23
Denominator: √(4² + 7²) = √(16 + 49) = √65

Finally, dividing the numerator by the denominator gives the distance:

Distance = 23 / √65
</think>

To find the distance from the point (1, 2) to the line \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to a line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients from the line equation \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\):**
   - \(A = 4\)
   - \(B = 7\)
   - \(C = 5\)

2. **Substitute the point (1, 2) into the formula:**
   \[
   |A x_0 + B y_0 + C| = |4(1) + 7(2) + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23
   \]

3. **Calculate the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{A^2 + B^2} = \sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}
   \]

4. **Compute the distance:**
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{23}{\sqrt{65}}}
\]

The distance from the point \((4, 1)\) to the line \(4x - y = 0\) can be calculated using either of two methods:

1. **Distance Formula Method**:
   The formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:
   \[
   d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]
   Here, the line can be rewritten as \(4x - y = 0\), so \(A=4\), \(B=-1\), and \(C=0\). Plugging in the point \((4, 1)\):
   \[
   d = \frac{|4(4) - 1(1)|}{\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{|16 - 1|}{\sqrt{17}} = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}}
   \]
   Rationalizing the denominator:
   \[
   d = \frac{15 \sqrt{17}}{17}
   \]

2. **Intersection Point Method**:
   The foot of the perpendicular from the point to the line is found by solving the system of equations formed by the given line and its perpendicular through the point.

   - The original line: \(y = 4x\)
   - The perpendicular line through \((4,1)\): using slope \(-1/4\), we get \(y - 1 = (-1/4)(x - 4)\) → simplifies to \(y = (-1/4)x + 2\)

   Solving for the intersection:
   \[
   4x = (-1/4)x + 2 \Rightarrow 17x = 8 \Rightarrow x = \frac{8}{17}, \quad y = 4(\frac{8}{17}) = \frac{32}{17}
   \]
   
   The foot of the perpendicular is at \((\frac{8}{17}, \frac{32}{17})\).

   Using the distance formula between \((4,1)\) and \((\frac{8}{17}, \frac{32}{17})\):
   \[
   d = \sqrt{\left(4 - \frac{8}{17}\right)^2 + \left(1 - \frac{32}{17}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{60}{17}\right)^2 + \left(-\frac{15}{17}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\frac{3825}{289}} = \frac{15\sqrt{17}}{17}
   \]

Both methods confirm the same distance.

**Answer:** The distance is \(\boxed{\dfrac{15\,\sqrt{17}}{17}}\) units.

The given linear equation is \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\). To analyze this, we can express it in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):

1. **Solving for y**:
   \[
   3x + 2y + 6 = 0 \\
   2y = -3x - 6 \\
   y = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3
   \]
   
2. **Key Features**:
   - **Slope (m)**: \(-\frac{3}{2}\)
   - **Y-intercept**: \(b = -3\) at the point \((0, -3)\).
   - **X-intercept**: Set \(y = 0\):
     \[
     0 = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3 \\
     x = -2
     \]
     So, the x-intercept is at \((-2, 0)\).

**Final Answer:**
The equation represents a line with slope \(-\frac{3}{2}\), y-intercept at \((0, -3)\), and x-intercept at \((-2, 0)\).  
\(\boxed{\text{Slope} = -\frac{3}{2}, \text{Y-intercept} = (0, -3), \text{X-intercept} = (-2, 0)}\)
